========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m here because I’m having a really hard time finishing things all the way through at work, even things I know are important. I work in meat processing, and there are these sanitation steps, batch logs, and end-of-line paperwork that have to be done correctly, and I’ll start them, I’ll really think I’m doing them, but somehow I leave off the last part or miss the final steps.

It’s not that I don’t care, because I do—honestly, I get stressed about it because I know my job depends on doing those things right. But I’ll get partway through and then it’s like I move on too soon or lose the thread, and later I realize I didn’t actually finish the whole thing. It’s been happening enough that I felt like I needed to come in and talk to someone, because I don’t know why I keep doing that when I know better.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… the biggest place I really notice it, and the reason I came in, is at work. That’s where it feels like it’s causing actual consequences. I’ll start the sanitation steps or I’ll be working through the batch logs or the end-of-line paperwork, and I honestly think I’m doing it properly, like I know these things matter and I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. But then somehow I leave out the last parts, or I don’t complete the final step, and I only realize later that it wasn’t actually finished.

It’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m choosing not to do it or thinking it’s unimportant. I’ll be partway through, and then something shifts and I’ve moved on, and the task is just… not all the way done. That’s the main thing I can point to clearly.

But I’ll get partway through and then it’s like I move on too soon or lose the thread, and later I realize I didn’t actually finish the whole thing. It’s been happening enough that I felt like I needed to come in and talk to someone, because I don’t know why I keep doing that when I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble finishing things or losing track of tasks outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the biggest place I really notice it, and the reason I came in, is at work. I’ll start the sanitation steps or I’ll be working through the batch logs or the end-of-line paperwork, and I honestly think I’m doing it properly, like I know these things matter and I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. I’ll be partway through, and then something shifts and I’ve moved on, and the task is just… not all the way done. That’s the main thing I can point to clearly.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any problems in your personal relationships due to these difficulties with finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t noticed it in the same clear way I notice it at work. Like, with work there are specific steps and paperwork, so it’s obvious when I didn’t finish something. In my personal life, it’s harder for me to tell if it’s causing problems with people or if I’m just kind of overthinking things.\n\nI don’t have a specific relationship issue that comes to mind where someone has said, “You never finish things,” or anything like that. I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed major problems there, at least not enough that I can point to it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind that you can't seem to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I get stressed about things, especially if I know I messed something up or I’m worried I forgot a step, and I might think about it a lot afterward. But I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts repeating that I can’t get rid of.\n\nLike, I don’t have a specific thought or phrase that keeps looping in my head over and over for no reason, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more just normal worrying when something has gone wrong, I think. If you mean like intrusive thoughts or something that feels stuck no matter what I do, I don’t really think that’s happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can fidget a little like anyone, like shifting in a chair or messing with my hands if I’m sitting for a while, but I don’t really feel like I’m constantly driven to move or like I physically can’t sit still.\n\nAt work I’m on my feet a lot anyway, so it’s kind of hard to compare, because I’m moving around as part of the job. But if I’m sitting somewhere, like here or at home, I don’t usually feel like I have to jump up or pace around. It’s more just normal worrying when something has gone wrong, I think. If you mean like intrusive thoughts or something that feels stuck no matter what I do, I don’t really think that’s happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can fidget a little like anyone, like shifting in a chair or messing with my hands if I’m sitting for a while, but I don’t really feel like I’m constantly driven to move or like I physically can’t sit still.\n\nAt work I’m on my feet a lot anyway, so it’s kind of hard to compare, because I’m moving around as part of the job. But if I’m sitting somewhere, like here or at home, I don’t usually feel like I have to jump up or pace around. So… no, I haven’t really noticed restlessness or fidgetiness as one of the main problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines for certain things, especially because at work there are things you’re supposed to do a certain way, but I don’t think I have anything where I feel like I have to repeat it over and over or something bad will happen.\n\nAt home, maybe I’ll double-check something once in a while, like whether I have my phone or keys, but it doesn’t feel like a ritual. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here. Outside of the actual cutting or processing part, it’s the follow-through stuff that gets me, especially when there are steps that have to be finished in order.

Like with sanitation steps, or batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, I’ll start it, and I really do know it matters. It’s not like I don’t care or I don’t understand that it has to be done correctly. But somehow I’ll get through part of it and then leave off the last section, or miss the final piece, or think I completed it and then later realize I didn’t actually finish the whole thing.

And that’s really frustrating, because it’s the kind of thing where my job depends on doing it right. I know the instructions, and if someone asks me what I’m supposed to do, I can usually explain it. But in the moment, following it all the way through without dropping the last part is where I mess up. It feels like I’m constantly starting the process properly and then somehow not landing the plane, if that makes sense.

through_Difficulty\n  description: Often does not follow through on instructions and fails to finish schoolwork, chores, or duties in the workplace.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that I keep description: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n  manifestation: I’m constantly losing things I need on the floor, like my cut glove, marker, badge, or safety glasses, and I’ll swear I just had them in my hand before I spend ten minutes retracing my steps.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n I feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of the reliable worker I want to be.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been a serious problem since I switched into full-time meat processing about two years and four months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S004\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines for certain things, especially because at work there are things you’re supposed to do a certain way, but I don’t think I have anything where I feel like I have to repeat it over and over or something bad will happen.\n\nAt home, maybe I’ll double-check something once in a while, like whether I have my phone or keys, but it doesn’t feel like a ritual. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it depends what you mean by organizing. Like, if you mean do I have a planner system or a neat way I lay everything out, then… not really, honestly. I try to, but it doesn’t always stay that way.

I can usually understand what needs to happen, and I can sometimes make a list or tell myself, “Okay, first I’ll do this, then this, then this.” But actually keeping the tasks sorted in my head and moving through them in a clean order is harder. I’ll start one thing, then realize another thing is also important, then I kind of shift to that, and then I’m not always clear what I already finished versus what I only meant to finish.

Even at home, if I’m trying to get chores done or run errands, I can spend a lot of time just sort of circling the tasks instead of doing them in order. I’ll think, “I should do laundry,” but then I need to clear the floor, and then I notice dishes, and then I remember I was supposed to text someone back, and then somehow the original thing is still sitting there. It’s not that I’m trying to be disorganized, and I don’t feel like I’m lazy exactly—it feels more like I can’t keep the structure of the task together unless it’s really obvious or someone else is directing it.

So yes, I think I do have trouble organizing tasks and activities, but I guess I notice it most when there are multiple steps or when no one is telling me exactly what to do next.

I’ll think, “I should do laundry,” but then I need to clear the floor, and then I notice dishes, and then I remember I was supposed to text someone back, and then somehow the original thing is still sitting there. It’s not that I’m trying to be disorganized, and I don’t feel like I’m lazy exactly—it feels more like I can’t keep the structure of the task together unless it’s really obvious or someone else is directing it.\n\nSo yes, I think I do have trouble organizing tasks and activities, but I guess I notice it most when there are multiple steps or when no one is telling me exactly what to do next.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not sure I’d say “often” in a big, constant way. I do feel guilty sometimes, like if I realize I forgot something or I think I made things harder for someone else. Then I’ll kind of replay it in my head and feel bad about it.

If you mean like intrusive thoughts or something that feels stuck no matter what I do, I don’t really think that’s happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can fidget a little like anyone, like shifting in a chair or messing with my hands if I’m sitting for a while, but I don’t really feel like I’m constantly driven to move or like I physically can’t sit still.\n\nAt work I’m on my feet a lot anyway, so it’s kind of hard to compare, because I’m moving around as part of the job. But if I’m sitting somewhere, like here or at home, I don’t usually feel like I have to jump up or pace around. So… no, I haven’t really noticed restlessness or fidgetiness as one of the main problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines for certain things, especially because at work there are things you’re supposed to do a certain way, but I don’t think I have anything where I feel like I have to repeat it over and over or something bad will happen.\n\nAt home, maybe I’ll double-check something once in a while, like whether I have my phone or keys, but it doesn’t feel like a ritual. It’s more just me trying not to forget something. I’m not stuck washing my hands repeatedly or checking locks again and again or counting things, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed anything like compulsive habits or rituals.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or nervous?\n\nPatient: I mean… I get nervous sometimes, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m anxious all the time or anything like that. It kind of depends on what’s going on. Like if there’s something important happening, or if I think I might have done something wrong, I can definitely feel tense and keep thinking about it for a while.\n\nBut as a general thing, like walking around every day feeling nervous for no reason, I don’t think that’s really me. I’m not usually sitting there with my heart racing or feeling panicky. It’s more situation-based, I guess. If there’s a reason to be stressed, then I feel stressed, but I don’t think I’m constantly anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention or following instructions in your daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here. Outside of the actual cutting or processing part, it’s the follow-through stuff that gets me, especially when there are steps that have to be finished in order.\n\nLike with sanitation steps, or batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, I’ll start it, and I really do know it matters. It’s not like I don’t care or I don’t understand that it has to be done correctly. But somehow I’ll get through part of it and then leave off the last section, or miss the final piece, or think I completed it and then later realize I didn’t actually finish the whole thing.\n\nAnd that’s really frustrating, because it’s the kind of thing where my job depends on doing it right. I know the instructions, and if someone asks me what I’m supposed to do, I can usually explain it. But in the moment, following it all the way through without dropping the last part is where I mess up. It feels like I’m constantly starting the process properly and then somehow not landing the plane, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it depends what you mean by organizing. Like, if you mean do I have a planner system or a neat way I lay everything out, then… not really, honestly. I try to, but it doesn’t always stay that way.\n\nI can usually understand what needs to happen, and I can sometimes make a list or tell myself, “Okay, first I’ll do this, then this, then this.” But actually keeping the tasks sorted in my head and moving through them in a clean order is harder. I’ll start one thing, then realize another thing is also important, then I kind of shift to that, and then I’m not always clear what I already finished versus what I only meant to finish.\n\nEven at home, if I’m trying to get chores done or run errands, I can spend a lot of time just sort of circling the tasks instead of doing them in order. I’ll think, “I should do laundry,” but then I need to clear the floor, and then I notice dishes, and then I remember I was supposed to text someone back, and then somehow the original thing is still sitting there. It’s not that I’m trying to be disorganized, and I don’t feel like I’m lazy exactly—it feels more like I can’t keep the structure of the task together unless it’s really obvious or someone else is directing it.\n\nSo yes, I think I do have trouble organizing tasks and activities, but I guess I notice it most when there are multiple steps or when no one is telling me exactly what to do next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often” in a big, constant way. I do feel guilty sometimes, like if I realize I forgot something or I think I made things harder for someone else. Then I’ll kind of replay it in my head and feel bad about it.\n\nBut I don’t think I walk around feeling guilty all the time, or like everything is my fault. It’s more tied to specific things that happen. If I know I messed something up, or even if I’m worried I might have, then yeah, I can feel guilty about that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I did. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early, not just something that started as an adult.

My mom actually still has some of my old elementary school report cards, and I remember seeing one from around fourth grade where the teacher basically said I was bright, but I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork and turn in homework. That’s kind of the exact pattern I remember, too. I wasn’t usually confused by the lesson itself. Like, if the teacher explained something and I was actually tuned in, I could understand it. But then when it came time to follow all the directions, or finish the worksheet, or remember to put the homework in the right place and actually hand it in, that’s where things would fall apart.

I remember missing parts of instructions a lot unless the teacher was nearby or kind of checking on me. If she was close to me, I could stay on track better, but if she gave the whole class directions and then moved on, I’d realize halfway through that I had missed a chunk of what we were supposed to do. It was embarrassing, because I didn’t feel like I didn’t know the material. It was more like I lost pieces of the process somewhere between hearing it and actually completing it.

It’s not that I’m trying to be disorganized, and I don’t feel like I’m lazy exactly—it feels more like I can’t keep the structure of the task together unless it’s really obvious or someone else is directing it.\n\nSo yes, I think I do have trouble organizing tasks and activities, but I guess I notice it most when there are multiple steps or when no one is telling me exactly what to do next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often” in a big, constant way. I do feel guilty sometimes, like if I realize I forgot something or I think I made things harder for someone else. Then I’ll kind of replay it in my head and feel bad about it.\n\nBut I don’t think I walk around feeling guilty all the time, or like everything is my fault. It’s more tied to specific things that happen. If I know I messed something up, or even if I’m worried I might have, then yeah, I can feel guilty about that. But not out of nowhere, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or completing assignments when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early, not just something that started as an adult.\n\nMy mom actually still has some of my old elementary school report cards, and I remember seeing one from around fourth grade where the teacher basically said I was bright, but I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork and turn in homework. That’s kind of the exact pattern I remember, too. I wasn’t usually confused by the lesson itself. Like, if the teacher explained something and I was actually tuned in, I could understand it. But then when it came time to follow all the directions, or finish the worksheet, or remember to put the homework in the right place and actually hand it in, that’s where things would fall apart.\n\nI remember missing parts of instructions a lot unless the teacher was nearby or kind of checking on me. If she was close to me, I could stay on track better, but if she gave the whole class directions and then moved on, I’d realize halfway through that I had missed a chunk of what we were supposed to do. I’m not really sure that fits me, honestly. I don’t remember being the kind of kid who was constantly running around or seemed like I had endless energy. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a really hard time finishing things all the way through at work, even things I know are important. I work in meat processing, and there are these sanitation steps, batch logs, and end-of-line paperwork that have to be done correctly, and I’ll start them, I’ll really think I’m doing them, but somehow I leave off the last part or miss the final steps.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care, because I do—honestly, I get stressed about it because I know my job depends on doing those things right. But I’ll get partway through and then it’s like I move on too soon or lose the thread, and later I realize I didn’t actually finish the whole thing. It’s been happening enough that I felt like I needed to come in and talk to someone, because I don’t know why I keep doing that when I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble finishing things or losing track of tasks outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the biggest place I really notice it, and the reason I came in, is at work. That’s where it feels like it’s causing actual consequences. I’ll start the sanitation steps or I’ll be working through the batch logs or the end-of-line paperwork, and I honestly think I’m doing it properly, like I know these things matter and I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. But then somehow I leave out the last parts, or I don’t complete the final step, and I only realize later that it wasn’t actually finished.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m choosing not to do it or thinking it’s unimportant. I’ll be partway through, and then something shifts and I’ve moved on, and the task is just… not all the way done. That’s the main thing I can point to clearly.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any problems in your personal relationships due to these difficulties with finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t noticed it in the same clear way I notice it at work. Like, with work there are specific steps and paperwork, so it’s obvious when I didn’t finish something. In my personal life, it’s harder for me to tell if it’s causing problems with people or if I’m just kind of overthinking things.\n\nI don’t have a specific relationship issue that comes to mind where someone has said, “You never finish things,” or anything like that. I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed major problems there, at least not enough that I can point to it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind that you can't seem to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I get stressed about things, especially if I know I messed something up or I’m worried I forgot a step, and I might think about it a lot afterward. But I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts repeating that I can’t get rid of.\n\nLike, I don’t have a specific thought or phrase that keeps looping in my head over and over for no reason, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more just normal worrying when something has gone wrong, I think. If you mean like intrusive thoughts or something that feels stuck no matter what I do, I don’t really think that’s happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can fidget a little like anyone, like shifting in a chair or messing with my hands if I’m sitting for a while, but I don’t really feel like I’m constantly driven to move or like I physically can’t sit still.\n\nAt work I’m on my feet a lot anyway, so it’s kind of hard to compare, because I’m moving around as part of the job. But if I’m sitting somewhere, like here or at home, I don’t usually feel like I have to jump up or pace around. So… no, I haven’t really noticed restlessness or fidgetiness as one of the main problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines for certain things, especially because at work there are things you’re supposed to do a certain way, but I don’t think I have anything where I feel like I have to repeat it over and over or something bad will happen.\n\nAt home, maybe I’ll double-check something once in a while, like whether I have my phone or keys, but it doesn’t feel like a ritual. It’s more just me trying not to forget something. I’m not stuck washing my hands repeatedly or checking locks again and again or counting things, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed anything like compulsive habits or rituals.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or nervous?\n\nPatient: I mean… I get nervous sometimes, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m anxious all the time or anything like that. It kind of depends on what’s going on. Like if there’s something important happening, or if I think I might have done something wrong, I can definitely feel tense and keep thinking about it for a while.\n\nBut as a general thing, like walking around every day feeling nervous for no reason, I don’t think that’s really me. I’m not usually sitting there with my heart racing or feeling panicky. It’s more situation-based, I guess. If there’s a reason to be stressed, then I feel stressed, but I don’t think I’m constantly anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention or following instructions in your daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here. Outside of the actual cutting or processing part, it’s the follow-through stuff that gets me, especially when there are steps that have to be finished in order.\n\nLike with sanitation steps, or batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, I’ll start it, and I really do know it matters. It’s not like I don’t care or I don’t understand that it has to be done correctly. But somehow I’ll get through part of it and then leave off the last section, or miss the final piece, or think I completed it and then later realize I didn’t actually finish the whole thing.\n\nAnd that’s really frustrating, because it’s the kind of thing where my job depends on doing it right. I know the instructions, and if someone asks me what I’m supposed to do, I can usually explain it. But in the moment, following it all the way through without dropping the last part is where I mess up. It feels like I’m constantly starting the process properly and then somehow not landing the plane, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it depends what you mean by organizing. Like, if you mean do I have a planner system or a neat way I lay everything out, then… not really, honestly. I try to, but it doesn’t always stay that way.\n\nI can usually understand what needs to happen, and I can sometimes make a list or tell myself, “Okay, first I’ll do this, then this, then this.” But actually keeping the tasks sorted in my head and moving through them in a clean order is harder. I’ll start one thing, then realize another thing is also important, then I kind of shift to that, and then I’m not always clear what I already finished versus what I only meant to finish.\n\nEven at home, if I’m trying to get chores done or run errands, I can spend a lot of time just sort of circling the tasks instead of doing them in order. I’ll think, “I should do laundry,” but then I need to clear the floor, and then I notice dishes, and then I remember I was supposed to text someone back, and then somehow the original thing is still sitting there. It’s not that I’m trying to be disorganized, and I don’t feel like I’m lazy exactly—it feels more like I can’t keep the structure of the task together unless it’s really obvious or someone else is directing it.\n\nSo yes, I think I do have trouble organizing tasks and activities, but I guess I notice it most when there are multiple steps or when no one is telling me exactly what to do next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often” in a big, constant way. I do feel guilty sometimes, like if I realize I forgot something or I think I made things harder for someone else. Then I’ll kind of replay it in my head and feel bad about it.\n\nBut I don’t think I walk around feeling guilty all the time, or like everything is my fault. It’s more tied to specific things that happen. If I know I messed something up, or even if I’m worried I might have, then yeah, I can feel guilty about that. But not out of nowhere, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or completing assignments when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early, not just something that started as an adult.\n\nMy mom actually still has some of my old elementary school report cards, and I remember seeing one from around fourth grade where the teacher basically said I was bright, but I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork and turn in homework. That’s kind of the exact pattern I remember, too. I wasn’t usually confused by the lesson itself. Like, if the teacher explained something and I was actually tuned in, I could understand it. But then when it came time to follow all the directions, or finish the worksheet, or remember to put the homework in the right place and actually hand it in, that’s where things would fall apart.\n\nI remember missing parts of instructions a lot unless the teacher was nearby or kind of checking on me. If she was close to me, I could stay on track better, but if she gave the whole class directions and then moved on, I’d realize halfway through that I had missed a chunk of what we were supposed to do. It was embarrassing, because I didn’t feel like I didn’t know the material. It was more like I lost pieces of the process somewhere between hearing it and actually completing it.\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel like you were constantly on the go or had a lot of excess energy when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that fits me, honestly. I don’t remember being the kind of kid who was constantly running around or seemed like I had endless energy. I mean, I was a child, so I’m sure I had normal kid energy at times, like playing or getting excited, but I don’t think people described me as always “on the go” or unable to slow down.\n\nIf anything, when I think back, that’s not the part that stands out. I wasn’t usually the kid bouncing off the walls or needing to move nonstop. I’d have to ask my mom to be totally sure, because childhood memories are kind of blurry, but from what I remember, no, I don’t think I had a lot of excess energy in that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Inattentive Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often” in a big, constant way. I do feel guilty sometimes, like if I realize I forgot something or I think I made things harder for someone else. Then I’ll kind of replay it in my head and feel bad about it.\n\nBut I don’t think I walk around feeling guilty all the time, or like everything is my fault. It’s more tied to specific things that happen. If I know I messed something up, or even if I’m worried I might have, then yeah, I can feel guilty about that. But not out of nowhere, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or completing assignments when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early, not just something that started as an adult.\n\nMy mom actually still has some of my old elementary school report cards, and I remember seeing one from around fourth grade where the teacher basically said I was bright, but I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork and turn in homework. That’s kind of the exact pattern I remember, too. I wasn’t usually confused by the lesson itself. Like, if the teacher explained something and I was actually tuned in, I could understand it. But then when it came time to follow all the directions, or finish the worksheet, or remember to put the homework in the right place and actually hand it in, that’s where things would fall apart.\n\nI remember missing parts of instructions a lot unless the teacher was nearby or kind of checking on me. If she was close to me, I could stay on track better, but if she gave the whole class directions and then moved on, I’d realize halfway through that I had missed a chunk of what we were supposed to do. It was embarrassing, because I didn’t feel like I didn’t know the material. It was more like I lost pieces of the process somewhere between hearing it and actually completing it.\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel like you were constantly on the go or had a lot of excess energy when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that fits me, honestly. I don’t remember being the kind of kid who was constantly running around or seemed like I had endless energy. I mean, I was a child, so I’m sure I had normal kid energy at times, like playing or getting excited, but I don’t think people described me as always “on the go” or unable to slow down.\n\nIf anything, when I think back, that’s not the part that stands out. I wasn’t usually the kid bouncing off the walls or needing to move nonstop. I’d have to ask my mom to be totally sure, because childhood memories are kind of blurry, but from what I remember, no, I don’t think I had a lot of excess energy in that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your difficulties with finishing tasks and following through on instructions are causing significant problems in your life outside of work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"At work this has led to formal retraining meetings, coworkers covering parts of my duties, and real fear that I’ll be moved off the line or fired. I feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of the reliable worker I want to be.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… yes, definitely. The biggest place I notice it is work, honestly, and it’s gotten to the point where it feels serious, not just annoying or like “oh, I’m forgetful.”

I work in a setting where there are steps and instructions that actually matter, and when I don’t finish something all the way or I miss part of what I was told, it doesn’t just affect me. Other people end up having to step in and cover pieces of my duties, or double-check things I should have already done. And that’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be careless. I really want to be reliable.

There have been formal retraining meetings because of it, where they go back over procedures with me, and I’ll understand it in the meeting, but then in the actual flow of work I’ll lose track of some part of it or not follow through the way I’m supposed to. It makes me feel like people are watching me like I’m a problem.

And I’m honestly scared I could be moved off the line or even fired if it keeps happening. That’s probably the part that weighs on me the most. I don’t want to be seen as a safety concern, but that’s kind of how it feels sometimes, like instead of being the dependable worker I’m trying to be, I’m becoming someone everyone has to work around.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F42.2, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a really hard time finishing things all the way through at work, even things I know are important. I work in meat processing, and there are these sanitation steps, batch logs, and end-of-line paperwork that have to be done correctly, and I’ll start them, I’ll really think I’m doing them, but somehow I leave off the last part or miss the final steps.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care, because I do—honestly, I get stressed about it because I know my job depends on doing those things right. But I’ll get partway through and then it’s like I move on too soon or lose the thread, and later I realize I didn’t actually finish the whole thing. It’s been happening enough that I felt like I needed to come in and talk to someone, because I don’t know why I keep doing that when I know better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble finishing things or losing track of tasks outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the biggest place I really notice it, and the reason I came in, is at work. That’s where it feels like it’s causing actual consequences. I’ll start the sanitation steps or I’ll be working through the batch logs or the end-of-line paperwork, and I honestly think I’m doing it properly, like I know these things matter and I know my job depends on finishing them correctly. But then somehow I leave out the last parts, or I don’t complete the final step, and I only realize later that it wasn’t actually finished.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I’m choosing not to do it or thinking it’s unimportant. I’ll be partway through, and then something shifts and I’ve moved on, and the task is just… not all the way done. That’s the main thing I can point to clearly.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any problems in your personal relationships due to these difficulties with finishing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t noticed it in the same clear way I notice it at work. Like, with work there are specific steps and paperwork, so it’s obvious when I didn’t finish something. In my personal life, it’s harder for me to tell if it’s causing problems with people or if I’m just kind of overthinking things.\n\nI don’t have a specific relationship issue that comes to mind where someone has said, “You never finish things,” or anything like that. I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed major problems there, at least not enough that I can point to it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind that you can't seem to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I get stressed about things, especially if I know I messed something up or I’m worried I forgot a step, and I might think about it a lot afterward. But I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts repeating that I can’t get rid of.\n\nLike, I don’t have a specific thought or phrase that keeps looping in my head over and over for no reason, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more just normal worrying when something has gone wrong, I think. If you mean like intrusive thoughts or something that feels stuck no matter what I do, I don’t really think that’s happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can fidget a little like anyone, like shifting in a chair or messing with my hands if I’m sitting for a while, but I don’t really feel like I’m constantly driven to move or like I physically can’t sit still.\n\nAt work I’m on my feet a lot anyway, so it’s kind of hard to compare, because I’m moving around as part of the job. But if I’m sitting somewhere, like here or at home, I don’t usually feel like I have to jump up or pace around. So… no, I haven’t really noticed restlessness or fidgetiness as one of the main problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines for certain things, especially because at work there are things you’re supposed to do a certain way, but I don’t think I have anything where I feel like I have to repeat it over and over or something bad will happen.\n\nAt home, maybe I’ll double-check something once in a while, like whether I have my phone or keys, but it doesn’t feel like a ritual. It’s more just me trying not to forget something. I’m not stuck washing my hands repeatedly or checking locks again and again or counting things, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed anything like compulsive habits or rituals.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or nervous?\n\nPatient: I mean… I get nervous sometimes, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m anxious all the time or anything like that. It kind of depends on what’s going on. Like if there’s something important happening, or if I think I might have done something wrong, I can definitely feel tense and keep thinking about it for a while.\n\nBut as a general thing, like walking around every day feeling nervous for no reason, I don’t think that’s really me. I’m not usually sitting there with my heart racing or feeling panicky. It’s more situation-based, I guess. If there’s a reason to be stressed, then I feel stressed, but I don’t think I’m constantly anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention or following instructions in your daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here. Outside of the actual cutting or processing part, it’s the follow-through stuff that gets me, especially when there are steps that have to be finished in order.\n\nLike with sanitation steps, or batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, I’ll start it, and I really do know it matters. It’s not like I don’t care or I don’t understand that it has to be done correctly. But somehow I’ll get through part of it and then leave off the last section, or miss the final piece, or think I completed it and then later realize I didn’t actually finish the whole thing.\n\nAnd that’s really frustrating, because it’s the kind of thing where my job depends on doing it right. I know the instructions, and if someone asks me what I’m supposed to do, I can usually explain it. But in the moment, following it all the way through without dropping the last part is where I mess up. It feels like I’m constantly starting the process properly and then somehow not landing the plane, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it depends what you mean by organizing. Like, if you mean do I have a planner system or a neat way I lay everything out, then… not really, honestly. I try to, but it doesn’t always stay that way.\n\nI can usually understand what needs to happen, and I can sometimes make a list or tell myself, “Okay, first I’ll do this, then this, then this.” But actually keeping the tasks sorted in my head and moving through them in a clean order is harder. I’ll start one thing, then realize another thing is also important, then I kind of shift to that, and then I’m not always clear what I already finished versus what I only meant to finish.\n\nEven at home, if I’m trying to get chores done or run errands, I can spend a lot of time just sort of circling the tasks instead of doing them in order. I’ll think, “I should do laundry,” but then I need to clear the floor, and then I notice dishes, and then I remember I was supposed to text someone back, and then somehow the original thing is still sitting there. It’s not that I’m trying to be disorganized, and I don’t feel like I’m lazy exactly—it feels more like I can’t keep the structure of the task together unless it’s really obvious or someone else is directing it.\n\nSo yes, I think I do have trouble organizing tasks and activities, but I guess I notice it most when there are multiple steps or when no one is telling me exactly what to do next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often” in a big, constant way. I do feel guilty sometimes, like if I realize I forgot something or I think I made things harder for someone else. Then I’ll kind of replay it in my head and feel bad about it.\n\nBut I don’t think I walk around feeling guilty all the time, or like everything is my fault. It’s more tied to specific things that happen. If I know I messed something up, or even if I’m worried I might have, then yeah, I can feel guilty about that. But not out of nowhere, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or completing assignments when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I do feel guilty sometimes, like if I realize I forgot something or I think I made things harder for someone else. Then I’ll kind of replay it in my head and feel bad about it.\n\nBut I don’t think I walk around feeling guilty all the time, or like everything is my fault. It’s more tied to specific things that happen. If I know I messed something up, or even if I’m worried I might have, then yeah, I can feel guilty about that. But not out of nowhere, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or completing assignments when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early, not just something that started as an adult.\n\nMy mom actually still has some of my old elementary school report cards, and I remember seeing one from around fourth grade where the teacher basically said I was bright, but I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork and turn in homework. That’s kind of the exact pattern I remember, too. I wasn’t usually confused by the lesson itself. Like, if the teacher explained something and I was actually tuned in, I could understand it. But then when it came time to follow all the directions, or finish the worksheet, or remember to put the homework in the right place and actually hand it in, that’s where things would fall apart.\n\nI remember missing parts of instructions a lot unless the teacher was nearby or kind of checking on me. If she was close to me, I could stay on track better, but if she gave the whole class directions and then moved on, I’d realize halfway through that I had missed a chunk of what we were supposed to do. It was embarrassing, because I didn’t feel like I didn’t know the material. It was more like I lost pieces of the process somewhere between hearing it and actually completing it.\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel like you were constantly on the go or had a lot of excess energy when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that fits me, honestly. I don’t remember being the kind of kid who was constantly running around or seemed like I had endless energy. I mean, I was a child, so I’m sure I had normal kid energy at times, like playing or getting excited, but I don’t think people described me as always “on the go” or unable to slow down.\n\nIf anything, when I think back, that’s not the part that stands out. I wasn’t usually the kid bouncing off the walls or needing to move nonstop. I’d have to ask my mom to be totally sure, because childhood memories are kind of blurry, but from what I remember, no, I don’t think I had a lot of excess energy in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with finishing tasks and following through on instructions are causing significant problems in your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, definitely. The biggest place I notice it is work, honestly, and it’s gotten to the point where it feels serious, not just annoying or like “oh, I’m forgetful.”\n\nI work in a setting where there are steps and instructions that actually matter, and when I don’t finish something all the way or I miss part of what I was told, it doesn’t just affect me. Other people end up having to step in and cover pieces of my duties, or double-check things I should have already done. And that’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be careless. I really want to be reliable.\n\nThere have been formal retraining meetings because of it, where they go back over procedures with me, and I’ll understand it in the meeting, but then in the actual flow of work I’ll lose track of some part of it or not follow through the way I’m supposed to. It makes me feel like people are watching me like I’m a problem.\n\nAnd I’m honestly scared I could be moved off the line or even fired if it keeps happening. That’s probably the part that weighs on me the most. I don’t want to be seen as a safety concern, but that’s kind of how it feels sometimes, like instead of being the dependable worker I’m trying to be, I’m becoming someone everyone has to work around.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not totally sure, honestly. I haven’t really connected it as clearly with friends or family the way I have with work.

Like, with family, I’m sure there are times where I forget something someone asked me to do, or I don’t follow through right away, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s caused major relationship problems. I guess my family might get annoyed with me sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as this big pattern in the same way.

So I guess my answer is I haven’t noticed it in a major way, or at least no one has really pointed it out to me like that.

I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed major problems there, at least not enough that I can point to it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind that you can't seem to get rid of?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I get stressed about things, especially if I know I messed something up or I’m worried I forgot a step, and I might think about it a lot afterward. But I don’t know if I’d call it thoughts repeating that I can’t get rid of.\n\nLike, I don’t have a specific thought or phrase that keeps looping in my head over and over for no reason, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more just normal worrying when something has gone wrong, I think. If you mean like intrusive thoughts or something that feels stuck no matter what I do, I don’t really think that’s happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can fidget a little like anyone, like shifting in a chair or messing with my hands if I’m sitting for a while, but I don’t really feel like I’m constantly driven to move or like I physically can’t sit still.\n\nAt work I’m on my feet a lot anyway, so it’s kind of hard to compare, because I’m moving around as part of the job. But if I’m sitting somewhere, like here or at home, I don’t usually feel like I have to jump up or pace around. So… no, I haven’t really noticed restlessness or fidgetiness as one of the main problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any habits or rituals that you feel compelled to do over and over?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have routines for certain things, especially because at work there are things you’re supposed to do a certain way, but I don’t think I have anything where I feel like I have to repeat it over and over or something bad will happen.\n\nAt home, maybe I’ll double-check something once in a while, like whether I have my phone or keys, but it doesn’t feel like a ritual. It’s more just me trying not to forget something. I’m not stuck washing my hands repeatedly or checking locks again and again or counting things, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed anything like compulsive habits or rituals.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or nervous?\n\nPatient: I mean… I get nervous sometimes, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m anxious all the time or anything like that. It kind of depends on what’s going on. Like if there’s something important happening, or if I think I might have done something wrong, I can definitely feel tense and keep thinking about it for a while.\n\nBut as a general thing, like walking around every day feeling nervous for no reason, I don’t think that’s really me. I’m not usually sitting there with my heart racing or feeling panicky. It’s more situation-based, I guess. If there’s a reason to be stressed, then I feel stressed, but I don’t think I’m constantly anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention or following instructions in your daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here. Outside of the actual cutting or processing part, it’s the follow-through stuff that gets me, especially when there are steps that have to be finished in order.\n\nLike with sanitation steps, or batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, I’ll start it, and I really do know it matters. It’s not like I don’t care or I don’t understand that it has to be done correctly. But somehow I’ll get through part of it and then leave off the last section, or miss the final piece, or think I completed it and then later realize I didn’t actually finish the whole thing.\n\nAnd that’s really frustrating, because it’s the kind of thing where my job depends on doing it right. I know the instructions, and if someone asks me what I’m supposed to do, I can usually explain it. But in the moment, following it all the way through without dropping the last part is where I mess up. It feels like I’m constantly starting the process properly and then somehow not landing the plane, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because it depends what you mean by organizing. Like, if you mean do I have a planner system or a neat way I lay everything out, then… not really, honestly. I try to, but it doesn’t always stay that way.\n\nI can usually understand what needs to happen, and I can sometimes make a list or tell myself, “Okay, first I’ll do this, then this, then this.” But actually keeping the tasks sorted in my head and moving through them in a clean order is harder. I’ll start one thing, then realize another thing is also important, then I kind of shift to that, and then I’m not always clear what I already finished versus what I only meant to finish.\n\nEven at home, if I’m trying to get chores done or run errands, I can spend a lot of time just sort of circling the tasks instead of doing them in order. I’ll think, “I should do laundry,” but then I need to clear the floor, and then I notice dishes, and then I remember I was supposed to text someone back, and then somehow the original thing is still sitting there. It’s not that I’m trying to be disorganized, and I don’t feel like I’m lazy exactly—it feels more like I can’t keep the structure of the task together unless it’s really obvious or someone else is directing it.\n\nSo yes, I think I do have trouble organizing tasks and activities, but I guess I notice it most when there are multiple steps or when no one is telling me exactly what to do next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often” in a big, constant way. I do feel guilty sometimes, like if I realize I forgot something or I think I made things harder for someone else. Then I’ll kind of replay it in my head and feel bad about it.\n\nBut I don’t think I walk around feeling guilty all the time, or like everything is my fault. It’s more tied to specific things that happen. If I know I messed something up, or even if I’m worried I might have, then yeah, I can feel guilty about that. But not out of nowhere, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or completing assignments when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I mean, looking back, it was there pretty early, not just something that started as an adult.\n\nMy mom actually still has some of my old elementary school report cards, and I remember seeing one from around fourth grade where the teacher basically said I was bright, but I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork and turn in homework. That’s kind of the exact pattern I remember, too. I wasn’t usually confused by the lesson itself. Like, if the teacher explained something and I was actually tuned in, I could understand it. But then when it came time to follow all the directions, or finish the worksheet, or remember to put the homework in the right place and actually hand it in, that’s where things would fall apart.\n\nI remember missing parts of instructions a lot unless the teacher was nearby or kind of checking on me. If she was close to me, I could stay on track better, but if she gave the whole class directions and then moved on, I’d realize halfway through that I had missed a chunk of what we were supposed to do. It was embarrassing, because I didn’t feel like I didn’t know the material. It was more like I lost pieces of the process somewhere between hearing it and actually completing it.\n\nDoctor: Did you often feel like you were constantly on the go or had a lot of excess energy when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that fits me, honestly. I don’t remember being the kind of kid who was constantly running around or seemed like I had endless energy. I mean, I was a child, so I’m sure I had normal kid energy at times, like playing or getting excited, but I don’t think people described me as always “on the go” or unable to slow down.\n\nIf anything, when I think back, that’s not the part that stands out. I wasn’t usually the kid bouncing off the walls or needing to move nonstop. I’d have to ask my mom to be totally sure, because childhood memories are kind of blurry, but from what I remember, no, I don’t think I had a lot of excess energy in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with finishing tasks and following through on instructions are causing significant problems in your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, definitely. The biggest place I notice it is work, honestly, and it’s gotten to the point where it feels serious, not just annoying or like “oh, I’m forgetful.”\n\nI work in a setting where there are steps and instructions that actually matter, and when I don’t finish something all the way or I miss part of what I was told, it doesn’t just affect me. Other people end up having to step in and cover pieces of my duties, or double-check things I should have already done. And that’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be careless. I really want to be reliable.\n\nThere have been formal retraining meetings because of it, where they go back over procedures with me, and I’ll understand it in the meeting, but then in the actual flow of work I’ll lose track of some part of it or not follow through the way I’m supposed to. It makes me feel like people are watching me like I’m a problem.\n\nAnd I’m honestly scared I could be moved off the line or even fired if it keeps happening. That’s probably the part that weighs on me the most. I don’t want to be seen as a safety concern, but that’s kind of how it feels sometimes, like instead of being the dependable worker I’m trying to be, I’m becoming someone everyone has to work around.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with friends or family that you think might be related to your difficulties with finishing tasks or following instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I haven’t really connected it as clearly with friends or family the way I have with work.\n\nLike, with family, I’m sure there are times where I forget something someone asked me to do, or I don’t follow through right away, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s caused major relationship problems. I guess my family might get annoyed with me sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as this big pattern in the same way.\n\nWith friends, I don’t know… I don’t think anyone has directly said, like, “you’re a bad friend because you don’t finish things” or anything like that. Maybe there are little things, like if I say I’ll look something up or handle a plan and then I forget, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. It’s harder for me to separate normal forgetfulness from something that’s actually affecting the relationship.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I haven’t noticed it in a major way, or at least no one has really pointed it out to me like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I haven’t really connected it as clearly with friends or family the way I have with work.\n\nLike, with family, I’m sure there are times where I forget something someone asked me to do, or I don’t follow through right away, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s caused major relationship problems. I guess my family might get annoyed with me sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as this big pattern in the same way.\n\nWith friends, I don’t know… I don’t think anyone has directly said, like, “you’re a bad friend because you don’t finish things” or anything like that. Maybe there are little things, like if I say I’ll look something up or handle a plan and then I forget, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. It’s harder for me to separate normal forgetfulness from something that’s actually affecting the relationship.\n\nSo I guess my answer is I haven’t noticed it in a major way, or at least no one has really pointed it out to me like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you often act on impulse when you were younger, doing things without thinking them through?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I don’t have a strong memory of being especially impulsive as a kid, at least not in the sense of constantly doing risky things or acting without any thought at all.

I mean, I’m sure I did normal kid things where I made a quick decision or didn’t think something through, because I think most kids do that sometimes. But if you’re asking whether it was a noticeable pattern, like people were always saying I was impulsive or I was always getting into trouble because I acted too fast, I don’t really remember that being the case.

It’s a little hard because childhood memories are fuzzy, and I don’t know what my parents or teachers would say if you asked them directly. But from what I can remember, I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who was constantly acting on impulse when I was younger.
